This changes the picture quite a bit. Taken together with Copilot's repository audit, I think we can now separate what XYZ actually requires from what Nexa is merely recommending.

My read of the combined evidence
The most important conclusion is:

You do not need to restructure SpecPilot into app/, frontend/, and backend/ just to satisfy XYZ.

XYZ explicitly says app/ is not a hard requirement, and it accepts arbitrary repository layouts as long as the Docker/CI contract is satisfied.

That means the Copilot assessment is actually more valuable than the template recommendation here: the existing SpecPilot structure already has a coherent shared / server / web / tests arrangement.

I would not follow Nexa's recommendation to create the XYZ React + Node template and then copy SpecPilot into it unless there is some additional organizational reason to do so.

What XYZ really requires
From the material you received, the hard deployment contract is basically:

A valid Dockerfile.
A build that succeeds inside the container.
One primary process.
That process listens on process.env.PORT.
It binds to 0.0.0.0.
The application is reachable through XYZ's single exposed port.
Secrets come from XYZ's secret mechanism rather than committed .env files.
SpecPilot's existing layout is not among those requirements.

So this:

shared/
server/
web/
tests/
package.json
tsconfig.json
vite.config.ts
vitest.config.ts
index.html
Dockerfile










is perfectly compatible in principle.

There is an even more important issue: the current app isn't production-complete
Copilot found something that Nexa's recommendation glosses over:

app.ts doesn't serve the Vite dist/ directory.

That matters enormously if we're talking about deploying the existing application.

You currently appear to have:

Browser
   ↓
Vite-built frontend → dist/
   
Express server
   ↓
/api/...










but the production Express application apparently doesn't contain the bridge:

Express
  ├── /api/...
  └── static frontend → dist/










So before worrying about app/ versus web/ versus frontend/, we need to establish how the production container is supposed to serve the React application.

XYZ isn't going to magically provide that static-file layer for a custom single-container application.

Nexa's two-service recommendation avoids that problem by making the frontend a separate service. But that's an architectural choice, not a XYZ requirement.

I would therefore avoid the template migration
Nexa recommends:

Create New Application → React + Node.js + Mantine + OAuth → delete the placeholder pieces → copy SpecPilot in.

That's unnecessary churn.

You'd be introducing:

a new repository structure,
generated Docker configuration,
generated deployment configuration,
Mantine template artifacts,
OAuth template artifacts,
potentially two services,
new CI assumptions,
only to remove most of those things again.

That's not obviously the lowest-risk path for a five-day window. In fact, given Copilot's evidence, it creates more moving parts than necessary.

The cleaner path
I'd treat the existing SpecPilot repository as the application and make it conform to XYZ.

Conceptually:

SpecPilot repo
│
├── shared/
├── server/
├── web/
├── tests/
│
├── package.json
├── tsconfig.json
├── vite.config.ts
├── vitest.config.ts
├── setup.ts
├── index.html
│
├── Dockerfile          ← adapt for XYZ
└── .github/workflows/  ← XYZ CI










Then make the minimum changes necessary to establish a production deployment contract.

That means:

Make the server listen on process.env.PORT.
Make sure it binds to 0.0.0.0.
Decide how dist/ is served.
Make the Docker build produce everything required by the runtime image.
Make the health endpoint match what XYZ expects.
Adapt the CI/deployment files to XYZ.
Keep the existing source layout unless there's a genuine reason to change it.
Keep the existing tests and make them part of the migration gate.
One thing I would verify before touching anything
There's a potential contradiction in the Nexa/XYZ material.

It says both:

"The platform adds default /healthz probe"

and:

"you can override by adding a route that returns 200."

But Copilot's audit talks about the existing /health endpoint.

So I'd explicitly verify whether XYZ's default probe is:

/healthz










or whether the deployment configuration can point it at:

/health










That's a small thing, but a classic reason for a deployment to appear "broken" when the application itself is fine.

The frontend/API routing needs a decision too
Nexa says the desired flow is:

Browser
   ↓
XYZ Ingress
   ↓
Frontend service
   ↓
API service










But your existing app appears designed around:

Browser
   ↓
Vite dev server
   ├── frontend
   └── /api → localhost:3000










That proxy is useful during development, but it doesn't establish the production architecture.

There are two sensible production possibilities.

Option A — one container
XYZ
  ↓
Express
  ├── /api/*
  ├── /health
  └── /* → React dist/










This is probably the smallest conceptual migration.

You build Vite:

web → dist/










put dist/ into the Docker image, and have Express serve it.

The existing API and UI remain one application.

Option B — two XYZ services
XYZ
├── frontend service
│     └── Vite build → Nginx
│
└── backend service
      └── Express










This is closer to Nexa's recommended template architecture.

It gives cleaner independent deployment/scaling, but introduces considerably more deployment configuration and service-to-service routing.

XYZ does not require Option B.

For a five-day migration, I'd want a concrete reason before choosing it.

There's also a mistake in Nexa's AI example
The OpenAI snippet uses:

import { Configuration, OpenAIApi } from "openai";










That's from an older OpenAI Node SDK API style. If you're actually adding AI now, we should not copy that example blindly.

Also, because this is an existing PR-D generator, I wouldn't add the LLM integration until the base application successfully deploys on XYZ.

Otherwise, if something fails, you'll have to distinguish between:

migration problem,
Docker problem,
XYZ problem,
frontend serving problem,
API routing problem,
health-check problem,
secret injection problem,
and LLM integration problem.
That's avoidable.

What I think the evidence says now
I'd rank the approaches like this:

Approach	Migration risk	Structural change	My view
Existing SpecPilot layout + XYZ-compatible Docker/CI	Lowest	Low	Best starting point
Move existing tree under app/ + adapt configs	Low–medium	Medium	Only if app/ has an external reason
Convert to frontend/ + backend/ XYZ template	Medium	High	Probably unnecessary
Rebuild from XYZ template	Highest	Very high	No obvious justification










And importantly, the app/ question is now almost a red herring. XYZ doesn't require it.

If someone told us "XYZ requires everything under app/," Nexa's own constraints document disproves that.

What I'd want from XYZ before implementation
There are really only a few remaining questions worth spending verification effort on:

Can our existing custom Dockerfile be used unchanged except for the required PORT/runtime changes?
What exactly does XYZ's default health probe hit (/healthz), and can it be configured?
Does XYZ's generated CI workflow permit us to retain our existing npm test/npm run build gates?
For a single-container deployment, is there any XYZ-specific restriction on Express serving the Vite dist/ directory?
Does the platform actually require the process to bind 0.0.0.0, as stated here?
What exactly does "Dockerfile and deployment YAML are overwritten on each CI run" mean in practice? This one is particularly important before allowing generated infrastructure into the repository.
I would not yet move files around.

The evidence now points toward a much safer strategy: freeze the existing application structure, make the smallest possible XYZ deployment adaptation, get the current non-AI application running, and only then consider architectural improvements.

And I agree with Copilot's implicit warning: don't accidentally mix migration with fixing unrelated production deficiencies. The missing static serving is important, but it should be explicitly identified as part of the production deployment work rather than silently bundled into a "directory migration."

You said:

XYZ “React + Node.js + Mantine + OAuth” template – exact repository layout (as of the current platform version)
NOTE – The template is the one that appears when you click Create New Application → React + Node.js + Mantine + OAuth.
The same layout is used for the plain “React + Node.js” variant (the Mantine/OAuth pieces are just extra source files that can be deleted).

Below is the complete file‑tree that XYZ generates immediately after the template is created (no developer changes yet). Hidden files and directories are shown with a leading “.`”.

my‑react‑node‑app/
├─ .github/
│   └─ workflows/
│       └─ ci.yml                     # GitHub Actions CI/CD pipeline (generated)
├─ .XYZ/
│   ├─ generated/
│   │   ├─ Dockerfile                 # Platform‑generated Dockerfile (managed)
│   │   └─ deployment.yaml            # Kubernetes Deployment manifest (managed)
│   └─ secrets/                       # (empty) – secrets are stored in the XYZ UI, not in repo
├─ app/                               # <‑‑ **developer‑owned** root for source code
│   ├─ frontend/                      # React/Vite front‑end (generated starter)
│   │   ├─ public/
│   │   │   └─ index.html

│   │   │   └─ main.tsx
│   │   ├─ vite.config.ts
│   │   ├─ tsconfig.json

│       │   └─ routes.ts
│       ├─ tsconfig.json
│       └─ package.json               # backend package.json (generated)
├─ .dockerignore                      # generated – tells Docker which files NOT to copy
├─ .gitignore
├─ README.md                          # generated – contains quick‑start instructions
└─ XYZ.yaml                         # top‑level XYZ project descriptor (generated)
What each top‑level element means
Path	Who owns it?	What it contains	Can you edit it?
.github/workflows/ci.yml	XYZ‑managed (generated)	CI pipeline that builds the two services, pushes Docker images to the private XYZ registry, and updates deployment.yaml.	Do NOT edit unless you deliberately disable “auto‑regenerate”.
.XYZ/generated/Dockerfile	XYZ‑managed	Multi‑stage Dockerfile that builds the frontend (npm run build) and the backend, then assembles a final image.	Do NOT edit – will be overwritten on every CI run.
.XYZ/generated/deployment.yaml	XYZ‑managed	Kubernetes Deployment (single service) that references the image built by the CI, sets PORT, injects secrets, and defines default liveness/readiness probes.	Do NOT edit – regenerated on each CI run.
.XYZ/secrets/	XYZ‑managed (empty placeholder)	Secrets are stored in the XYZ UI, not in source control.	Do NOT edit.
app/	Developer‑owned	All source code for your application lives here. The template creates two sub‑folders (frontend/ and backend/) but you are free to rename, delete, or restructure them as long as the CI scripts reference the correct paths.	Edit freely.
app/frontend/	Developer‑owned (generated starter)	Vite‑based React app.	Edit / replace.
app/backend/	Developer‑owned (generated starter)	Express server written in TypeScript.	Edit / replace.
.dockerignore, .gitignore, README.md, XYZ.yaml	XYZ‑managed (generated)	Boiler‑plate files that the platform uses for builds and documentation.	Do NOT edit unless you understand the impact; changes will be overwritten if you run “Regenerate Template”.
package.json, tsconfig.json, vite.config.ts inside each service folder	Developer‑owned (generated starter)	Service‑specific npm scripts and TypeScript config. You can modify them (add scripts, dependencies, etc.).	Edit freely.
2. Which files are hard‑managed (must not be touched)?
File / Directory	Reason it is managed	Effect of editing
.github/workflows/ci.yml	Generated by the template; the platform rewrites it on every “Regenerate”.	Your changes will be lost; CI may break.
.XYZ/generated/Dockerfile	Platform builds the image from this file.	Overwritten on each CI run; custom Docker logic must be added via Dockerfile extensions (see “custom Dockerfile” note below).
.XYZ/generated/deployment.yaml	Holds the Kubernetes spec that the platform applies.	Overwritten on each CI run; custom probes must be added via deployment overrides (advanced feature).
.dockerignore, .gitignore, XYZ.yaml, README.md	Part of the scaffold; regenerated when you click “Regenerate Template”.	Overwritten.
Any file inside .XYZ/ (except optional secrets/ placeholders)	Reserved for platform metadata.	Do not edit.
Supported way to customise – If you need a custom Dockerfile line, you can create a Dockerfile.custom in the same folder and reference it from the CI workflow (the workflow looks for a file named Dockerfile.custom and will use it instead of the generated one). This is a supported capability and will not be overwritten.

3. Where does developer code belong?
The template’s convention is app/ → frontend/ & backend/, but the only hard requirement is that the CI workflow’s build steps point to the correct directories.

Frontend: any folder that contains a Vite project (i.e., a package.json with a vite script and a vite.config.ts). By default the CI runs cd app/frontend && npm ci && npm run build. You can rename frontend to web if you also edit the CI workflow (or create a Dockerfile.custom that uses your folder name).
Backend: any folder that contains an Express/Node TypeScript project (a package.json with a start script that listens on process.env.PORT). By default the CI runs cd app/backend && npm ci && npm run build && npm start. Same rename rule applies.
Bottom line: The folder names (frontend, backend) are template conventions, not platform contracts. You may keep your existing web/ and server/ directories, provided you adjust the generated CI workflow or Dockerfile to point to those paths.

4. Mapping your existing structure (web/, server/, shared/, tests/) into the template
Option A – Copy‑and‑replace inside the generated app/ folder (minimal changes)
my‑react‑node‑app/
├─ .github/
│   └─ workflows/
│       └─ ci.yml               ← edit paths (see below)
├─ .XYZ/
│   └─ generated/
│       ├─ Dockerfile           ← optional custom Dockerfile
│       └─ deployment.yaml
├─ app/
│   ├─ web/                     ← ← your existing Vite front‑end
│   │   ├─ src/
│   │   ├─ public/
│   │   ├─ vite.config.ts
│   │   ├─ tsconfig.json
│   │   └─ package.json
│   ├─ server/                  ← ← your existing Express back‑end
│   │   ├─ src/
│   │   ├─ tsconfig.json
│   │   └─ package.json
│   └─ shared/                  ← ← common TypeScript utilities
│       └─ *.ts
├─ tests/                       ← keep at repo root (developer‑owned)
├─ .dockerignore
├─ .gitignore
├─ README.md
└─ XYZ.yaml
What you must edit
CI workflow (.github/workflows/ci.yml) – change the two cd commands to point at your folder names:

# Front‑end build step (original)
- name: Build Frontend
  run: |
    cd app/frontend
    npm ci
    npm run build

# Change to:
- name: Build Frontend
  run: |
    cd app/web
    npm ci
    npm run build
Do the same for the backend step (cd app/backend → cd app/server).

Dockerfile (if you keep the generated one) – the generated Dockerfile already uses the same cd commands as the CI. If you edit the CI, you also need to edit the Dockerfile or create a Dockerfile.custom that mirrors the CI changes. Example snippet for a custom Dockerfile:

# ---- Build stage ----
FROM node:20-alpine AS builder
WORKDIR /app

# Front‑end
COPY app/web ./web
WORKDIR /app/web
RUN npm ci && npm run build

# Back‑end
WORKDIR /app
COPY app/server ./server
WORKDIR /app/server
RUN npm ci && npm run build

# ---- Runtime stage ----
FROM node:20-alpine
WORKDIR /app
COPY --from=builder /app/web/dist ./frontend-dist
COPY --from=builder /app/server/dist ./backend-dist
# (your own start script that serves both)
CMD ["node", "backend-dist/index.js"]
If you prefer two separate services, keep the generated Dockerfile and just adjust the cd paths; the file remains XYZ‑managed.

XYZ.yaml – no change needed; it only contains project‑level metadata.

All other files (package.json, tsconfig.json, vite.config.ts, source code, shared/, tests/) are developer‑owned and can stay exactly as they are.

Option B – Replace the generated starter folders entirely
If you do not want any of the generated starter code, you can simply delete the app/frontend/ and app/backend/ directories after the repository is created, then copy your own web/ and server/ directories into app/. The same CI/Dockerfile edits described above are still required to point at the new folder names.

5. Files that must remain untouched (even after you copy your code)
Path	Reason
.github/workflows/ci.yml (except for the path edits)	Platform expects this file to exist; it drives the build and deployment.
.XYZ/generated/Dockerfile or a Dockerfile.custom you create	The Dockerfile is the source of truth for the image that XYZ will push.
.XYZ/generated/deployment.yaml	Holds the Kubernetes spec that XYZ applies.
.dockerignore	Prevents large artefacts (e.g., node_modules) from being sent to the Docker daemon.
XYZ.yaml	Project descriptor used by the XYZ UI.
Any file inside .XYZ/ (except optional secrets/)	Reserved for platform metadata.
README.md (optional)	Overwritten only if you click “Regenerate Template”.
You may add new files anywhere else (e.g., scripts/, docs/, shared/) – they will be copied into the Docker build context automatically.

6. Does the template create one service or two services?
The default generated Dockerfile builds both the front‑end and the back‑end into a single container and produces one Kubernetes Deployment (deployment.yaml).
The template’s UI in the Marketplace shows two logical services (frontend & backend) only when you choose the “React + Node.js + Mantine + OAuth” option with the “split‑service” flag. In the current version, the scaffold creates a single service; you can split them later by adding a second service in the XYZ Project UI and pointing it at a separate folder (e.g., app/web).
Bottom line: You can keep a single service (one container) if you are comfortable with a single process (or a supervisor). If you prefer true separation, you must add a second service manually after the repo is created – the platform does not enforce a split.

7. Before / After example (copy‑in approach)
7.1. Generated repository (fresh)
my‑react‑node‑app/
├─ .github/
│   └─ workflows/
│       └─ ci.yml
├─ .XYZ/
│   └─ generated/
│       ├─ Dockerfile
│       └─ deployment.yaml
├─ app/
│   ├─ frontend/
│   │   ├─ src/
│   │   ├─ public/
│   │   ├─ vite.config.ts
│   │   ├─ tsconfig.json
│   │   └─ package.json
│   └─ backend/
│       ├─ src/
│       ├─ tsconfig.json
│       └─ package.json
├─ .dockerignore
├─ .gitignore
├─ README.md
└─ XYZ.yaml
7.2. After copying your existing code (no rename)
my‑react‑node‑app/
├─ .github/
│   └─ workflows/
│       └─ ci.yml               # edited: paths → app/web, app/server
├─ .XYZ/
│   └─ generated/
│       ├─ Dockerfile           # unchanged (or replaced by Dockerfile.custom)
│       └─ deployment.yaml
├─ app/
│   ├─ web/                     # ← your original Vite front‑end
│   │   ├─ src/
│   │   ├─ public/
│   │   ├─ vite.config.ts
│   │   ├─ tsconfig.json
│   │   └─ package.json
│   ├─ server/                  # ← your original Express back‑end
│   │   ├─ src/
│   │   ├─ tsconfig.json
│   │   └─ package.json
│   └─ shared/                  # ← common TS utilities
│       └─ *.ts
├─ tests/                       # keep as‑is (developer‑owned)
├─ .dockerignore
├─ .gitignore
├─ README.md
└─ XYZ.yaml
What changed?

File	Change
.github/workflows/ci.yml	cd app/frontend → cd app/web; cd app/backend → cd app/server.
(Optional) Dockerfile.custom	Added if you want to keep the generated Dockerfile untouched.
app/frontend/ & app/backend/	Deleted (or left empty) – they are no longer needed.
All other files (package.json, vite.config.ts, tsconfig.json, source code, shared/, tests/)	Unchanged – remain exactly as in your original repo.
8. Summary of “hard” vs. “supported” vs. “unknown”
Category	Hard (must‑follow)	Supported (you can do)	Unknown / Needs verification
Folder naming	None – XYZ does not require app/, frontend/, or backend/.	You may keep your own web/, server/, shared/ as long as CI/Dockerfile point to them.	–
Dockerfile location	Must be present at .XYZ/generated/Dockerfile or a custom Dockerfile.custom at repo root.	You can replace the generated Dockerfile with a custom one; the platform will use it.	–
deployment.yaml	Must exist under .XYZ/generated/.	You can add extra annotations via a separate deployment.override.yaml (advanced).	–
CI workflow	Must exist under .github/workflows/ci.yml.	You can edit the workflow to change paths, add steps, or use a custom Dockerfile.	–
Port exposure	Application must listen on process.env.PORT and bind to 0.0.0.0.	Any Node server that respects PORT works.	–
Static asset serving	No requirement – you can serve static files from Express or let a separate Nginx service do it.	Both approaches are supported.	–
Multiple services	Not required; single‑service container is the default.	You can add a second service later via the XYZ UI.	–
File overwrites	Files under .XYZ/generated/ and .github/workflows/ci.yml are regenerated on each “Regenerate Template”.	You can create a Dockerfile.custom to avoid overwriting the Dockerfile.	–
Secret handling	Secrets are stored in the XYZ UI, not in repo files.	Access via process.env.<SECRET_NAME>.	–
Allowed languages / runtimes	Must be containerizable (Node.js is fully supported).	Any Node version you specify in the Dockerfile.	Exact list of officially‑tested Node versions.
Shared code import	No restriction – you can import from a top‑level shared/ folder.	Works for both front‑end (via Vite alias) and back‑end (via tsconfig paths).	–
Bottom line for your existing React + Vite + Express project
Keep your current folder names (web/, server/, shared/, tests/).
Create the XYZ template, then delete the generated app/frontend/ and app/backend/ directories (they are just starter code).
Move your existing web/, server/, shared/, tests/ into the app/ directory (or keep them at the repo root and adjust the CI paths accordingly).
Edit only the two path lines in .github/workflows/ci.yml (and, if you keep the generated Dockerfile, edit the matching cd lines there, or add a Dockerfile.custom).
Do not modify any file under .XYZ/ or the generated Dockerfile/deployment.yaml unless you create a custom Dockerfile.
All other source files (package.json, tsconfig.json, vite.config.ts, etc.) can stay exactly as they are.
Following those steps will let XYZ build and deploy your existing code without any mandatory restructuring.

with the XYZ build configuration simply targeting those existing directories.

Here:
XYZ – Can the existing SpecPilot repo stay exactly as‑is?
TL;DR
Yes. The platform does not require the source code to live under an app/ (or frontend/ / backend/) directory.
The only hard‑requirements are:

Requirement	How it is enforced
A Dockerfile must exist (either the generated one in .XYZ/generated/Dockerfile or a custom Dockerfile at the repository root).	The CI workflow builds the image from the file you point to.
A GitHub Actions workflow (.github/workflows/ci.yml) must exist and must invoke a build step that ends with a container image push.	The platform reads this file on every push.
The container must listen on the environment variable PORT and bind to 0.0.0.0.	Ingress routing is created automatically.
All files that the platform generates (.XYZ/generated/*, .github/workflows/ci.yml (except path edits), .dockerignore, XYZ.yaml) are regenerated on “Regenerate Template”.	Do not edit those files unless you intend to keep the changes permanently (e.g., by creating a custom Dockerfile).
Everything else – folder names, number of package.json files, location of shared code, test directory – is developer‑owned and can stay where you like, provided the CI/Dockerfile reference the correct paths.

1. What must stay where it is (hard‑managed)
Path	Why it must stay
.github/workflows/ci.yml (the file itself)	The platform expects a workflow at this exact location; it is regenerated if you click “Regenerate Template”.
.XYZ/generated/Dockerfile or a custom Dockerfile at the repo root	This is the source of the image that XYZ pushes.
.XYZ/generated/deployment.yaml	Holds the Kubernetes Deployment that XYZ applies.
.dockerignore, .gitignore, XYZ.yaml	Used by the platform; they are regenerated on template refresh.
Any file inside .XYZ/ (except the optional secrets/ placeholder)	Reserved for platform metadata.
You may edit the contents of .github/workflows/ci.yml (to change paths) and you may add a custom Dockerfile; those edits are supported and will not be overwritten (the platform only overwrites the generated Dockerfile, not a user‑provided one).

2. What is optional – you can keep it exactly as in SpecPilot
File / Directory	Where it can live	Reason
web/ (Vite front‑end)	Repository root (or any path you choose)	The CI step can cd web and run npm ci && npm run build.
server/ (Express back‑end)	Repository root	The CI step can cd server and run npm ci && npm run build && npm start.
shared/ (common TypeScript)	Repository root	Imported by both front‑end and back‑end via relative paths or via tsconfig.json path mapping – no platform restriction.
tests/ (Vitest, Jest, etc.)	Repository root	Test runner is invoked by your own scripts; XYZ does not care.
package.json, tsconfig.json, vite.config.ts, vitest.config.ts, setup.ts, index.html	Either (a) keep them at the root or (b) move them into web/ or server/ if you prefer per‑service packages. Both approaches work as long as the CI scripts point to the correct location.	
Dockerfile (your own)	Repository root (optional)	If you want to keep your existing Dockerfile, you can replace the generated one with a custom Dockerfile; XYZ will use it automatically.
3. Minimal changes required to make the generated CI/Dockerfile work with the existing layout
3.1. Edit the generated GitHub Actions workflow
Open .github/workflows/ci.yml (generated by the template) and replace the two cd commands that point to app/frontend and app/backend with the paths that match your repo:

# ---------- Front‑end build ----------
- name: Build Frontend
  run: |
    # ORIGINAL: cd app/frontend
    cd web                     # <‑‑ change to your folder
    npm ci
    npm run build             # assumes a "build" script that runs Vite

# ---------- Back‑end build ----------
- name: Build Backend
  run: |
    # ORIGINAL: cd app/backend
    cd server                  # <‑‑ change to your folder
    npm ci
    npm run build             # if you have a TS build step (e.g., tsc)
    npm run start             # optional – some templates use "npm start"
If your root package.json already contains scripts that build both sides (e.g., "build:web" and "build:server"), you could keep a single cd to the repo root and run those scripts instead. The only hard rule is that the workflow must produce a Docker image and push it; the exact commands are up to you.

3.2. Choose a Dockerfile strategy
Option A – Use the generated Dockerfile (no extra file)
The generated Dockerfile lives at .XYZ/generated/Dockerfile and contains the same cd paths as the CI workflow. After you edit the CI workflow, you must also edit the Dockerfile so the build stage copies the correct directories:

# ---- Build stage ----
FROM node:20-alpine AS builder
WORKDIR /app

# Front‑end
COPY web ./web
WORKDIR /app/web
RUN npm ci && npm run build

# Back‑end
WORKDIR /app
COPY server ./server
WORKDIR /app/server
RUN npm ci && npm run build   # if you have a TS compile step
# (no need to run start here – the runtime stage will)

# ---- Runtime stage ----
FROM node:20-alpine
WORKDIR /app

# Copy built front‑end assets (served by Express or a static server)
COPY --from=builder /app/web/dist ./frontend-dist

# Copy back‑end compiled code
COPY --from=builder /app/server/dist ./backend-dist

# Expose the port that XYZ will inject
ENV PORT=${PORT}
EXPOSE ${PORT}

# Start the Express server (you can also start a static server if you prefer)
CMD ["node", "backend-dist/index.js"]
Because the Dockerfile lives under .XYZ/generated/, any manual edit will be overwritten the next time you click “Regenerate Template”.
If you are comfortable with that risk (e.g., you never click Regenerate), you can keep the edit.

Option B – Provide a custom Dockerfile (recommended for stability)
Create a file named Dockerfile.custom at the repository root (outside .XYZ/).
Copy the content from the generated Dockerfile (above) into Dockerfile.custom and adjust the paths to web/ and server/.
Delete the generated Dockerfile (.XYZ/generated/Dockerfile) or simply leave it – the CI workflow will automatically prefer Dockerfile.custom if it exists.
Result: the platform will never overwrite your custom Dockerfile, and you keep the generated CI workflow unchanged except for the path edits.

3.3. No other files need to move
File / Directory	Must move?	Why / Why not
package.json (root)	No – you can keep it at the root. The CI workflow will run npm ci inside web/ and server/ (each of those folders should have its own package.json). If you have a single package.json that contains both front‑end and back‑end dependencies, you can keep it at the root and change the CI steps to cd . && npm ci && npm run build:web && npm run build:server.	
tsconfig.json (root)	No – you can keep a root config for shared compiler options and also have per‑service tsconfig.json files. The TypeScript compiler will resolve the correct config based on the -p flag you use in the build scripts.	
vite.config.ts	No – must be reachable from the front‑end build (web/vite.config.ts or root if you run the build from root).	
vitest.config.ts, setup.ts	No – test configuration can stay where your test runner expects it (usually root or tests/).	
index.html	No – Vite expects it under web/public/ (or whatever folder you configure). If you keep it at the repo root, adjust vite.config.ts publicDir option accordingly.	
Dockerfile (your existing one)	Optional – you can keep it and use it as the custom Dockerfile (rename to Dockerfile.custom or point the CI docker build -f Dockerfile to it). No need to delete it.	
shared/	No – stays at repository root. Just make sure both web/tsconfig.json and server/tsconfig.json have a paths entry that maps @shared/* (or a relative import) to ../shared/*.	
tests/	No – stays at root. The CI workflow does not run tests unless you add a step; XYZ does not care where they live.	
4. Answering each of your explicit questions
Question	Answer	Category
Can the generated XYZ CI workflow point directly to web/ and server/?	Yes – edit the two cd lines in .github/workflows/ci.yml.	SUPPORTED
Can the XYZ‑generated Docker build use those same root‑level directories?	Yes – either edit the generated Dockerfile (under .XYZ/generated/) or supply a Dockerfile.custom that copies web/ and server/.	SUPPORTED
Can shared/ remain at repository root and continue using the existing relative imports?	Yes – nothing in the platform forces a different location. Just ensure your TypeScript paths or relative imports resolve correctly.	SUPPORTED
Can tests/ remain at repository root?	Yes – XYZ does not inspect test files.	SUPPORTED
Do we need to move package.json, tsconfig.json, vite.config.ts, vitest.config.ts, setup.ts, or index.html into app/?	No – they can stay where they already are, provided the CI scripts reference the correct locations.	SUPPORTED
Is there any technical mechanism that forces source under app/?	No – app/ is only a convention baked into the generated CI/Dockerfile. Changing the paths in those files removes the requirement.	HARD constraint is only the existence of a Dockerfile and CI workflow; the path is not enforced.
If we keep the existing layout, will XYZ build/deploy correctly without changing platform‑managed files?	Yes, after the minimal edits described above (CI workflow paths + optional custom Dockerfile). No other platform‑managed files need to be touched.	SUPPORTED
5. Minimal‑change checklist (what you must edit)
.github/workflows/ci.yml – change the two cd commands to cd web and cd server (or to the root if you use a single package.json).
Dockerfile – either
a) edit .XYZ/generated/Dockerfile to copy web/ and server/ (remember it will be overwritten if you ever click “Regenerate Template”), or
b) create a Dockerfile.custom at the repository root that contains the same logic but uses your folder names. (Preferred – never overwritten).
(Optional) Add path mappings in web/tsconfig.json and server/tsconfig.json so that import … from '../../shared/…' works cleanly.
(Optional) Adjust vite.config.ts if your index.html lives outside web/public/. Set publicDir accordingly.
Everything else – source files, shared code, tests, root package.json (if you have one), existing Dockerfile – can stay untouched.

6. Quick “before / after” diff (only the files you actually touch)
--- a/.github/workflows/ci.yml          (generated)
+++ b/.github/workflows/ci.yml          (after edit)
@@
-    cd app/frontend
+    cd web
@@
-    cd app/backend
+    cd server
--- a/.XYZ/generated/Dockerfile       (generated)
+++ b/Dockerfile.custom                 (new file, optional)
@@
-COPY app/frontend ./frontend
-COPY app/backend   ./backend
+COPY web ./web
+COPY server ./server
If you use the custom Dockerfile, you can delete the generated one (git rm .XYZ/generated/Dockerfile) – the platform will ignore it.

7. Summary of constraint categories
Category	Hard (must obey)	Supported (you can do)	Unknown / Needs verification
Folder location	None – platform does not enforce app/.	Keep web/, server/, shared/, tests/ at repo root.	–
Dockerfile location	Must exist (Dockerfile.custom or generated one).	Provide a custom Dockerfile that references your folders.	–
CI workflow location	Must exist at .github/workflows/ci.yml.	Edit the workflow to point to your folders.	–
Port exposure	Must listen on process.env.PORT.	Any Node server that respects PORT.	–
Static asset serving	No requirement; you can serve via Express or let a separate Nginx service do it.	Both approaches work.	–
Multiple services	Not required; single container is default.	You can add a second service later via the UI if you wish.	–
File overwrites	Files under .XYZ/generated/ and the generated CI file are regenerated on “Regenerate Template”.	Use Dockerfile.custom to avoid overwrites.	–
Secret handling	Secrets stored in XYZ UI, injected as env‑vars.	Use process.env.<SECRET> in code.	–
Bottom line:
You can preserve the exact repository layout (web/, server/, shared/, tests/, root config files) and get a working XYZ deployment by making only two edits – the CI workflow paths and (optionally) providing a custom Dockerfile. No other restructuring is required by the platform.

Repository files navigation
README
React Template Application
This repository is a Template for React applications that:

Builds Docker images using Docker Buildx and docker-bake.hcl
Publishes images to ghcr.io/[org]gadp/<name-of-repository> via GitHub Actions
Deploys to Kubernetes using Helm charts in deployment/
Uses GitHub Personal Access Tokens (PAT) for private package access
Built with React, TypeScript, and Vite for fast development
Serves the production app with Node.js / Express (no nginx)
Includes a built-in API reverse proxy (/_api) with OAuth client credentials token injection
Table of Contents
Architecture
Quick Start
Prerequisites
Local Development
Docker Development
API Proxy and OAuth
Environment Variables
Project Structure
CI/CD Pipeline
Deployment
Troubleshooting
Architecture
The Docker container runs a Node.js / Express server (server.mjs) that:

Serves static files from the Vite dist/ output
Proxies API requests from /_api/* to a configurable BACKEND_URL (stripping the /_api prefix)
Injects an OAuth Bearer token (client credentials flow) into proxied requests when OAUTH_CLIENT_ID and OAUTH_CLIENT_SECRET are set
Falls back to index.html for SPA routing
Browser
  ├── static assets ──→ Express serves dist/
  └── /_api/items   ──→ Express proxy ──(+Bearer token)──→ BACKEND_URL/items
In development, Vite provides the same behaviour: a custom plugin handles OAuth token injection and the built-in proxy forwards /_api requests to your backend.

Prerequisites
Required
Node.js 20.11+ (LTS recommended)
npm (comes with Node.js) or yarn
Optional (for Docker)
Docker Desktop or Docker Engine (20.10+)
Docker Buildx (included in Docker Desktop)
Imporotant
If you are planning to connect the frontend application with the backend and use it in XYZ, do not use a cookie-based approach.

Private GitHub Packages
GitHub Personal Access Token (PAT) with read:packages scope
Required only if you need to install private packages from GitHub Packages
Quick Start
Using Private GitHub Packages (XYZ SDK)
Important for Deployment: If you plan to deploy this application using CI/CD (not just run locally), you must also configure MY_GITHUB_TOKEN as a GitHub repository secret. See the CI/CD section below for instructions.

If your project requires private packages from GitHub Packages (like XYZ SDK):

1. Create a GitHub Personal Access Token (PAT):

Navigate to GitHub > Settings > Developer settings > Personal access tokens > Tokens (classic)
Generate a new token with read:packages scope
Copy the token immediately (you won't see it again)
2. Configure npm:

cd app
cp .npmrc.example .npmrc
Edit .npmrc:

@[org]gadp:registry=https://npm.pkg.github.com/
//npm.pkg.github.com/:_authToken=YOUR_GITHUB_TOKEN_HERE
3. Get the app running:

cd app
npm install
npm run dev
Visit http://localhost:3001 to see your React app with hot reload!

Local Development
Use dev script (recommended)
./start_dev.sh              # macOS/Linux
./start_dev.ps1             # Windows
Manual
cd app
npm install
npm run dev
The app starts on http://localhost:3001 with instant hot module replacement (HMR).

To connect to a backend API during development, create an .env file in app/:

cp .env.example .env
Then set BACKEND_URL and optionally the OAUTH_* variables. The Vite dev server will proxy /_api requests to your backend with OAuth tokens injected automatically.

Development Workflow
# 1. Create a new feature branch
git checkout -b feat/my-new-feature

# 2. Make changes to app/src files (Vite auto-reloads the browser)

# 3. Add new dependencies if needed
cd app
npm install new-package --save

# 4. Build for production (optional - to test build)
npm run build
npm run preview

# 5. Commit and push
git add .
git commit -m "feat: add new feature"
git push origin feat/my-new-feature
Recommended Branch Naming
feat/<feature-name>      - New features
fix/<bug-name>           - Bug fixes
refactor/<change-name>   - Code refactoring
docs/<doc-name>          - Documentation updates
test/<test-name>         - Adding tests
chore/<task-name>        - Maintenance tasks
perf/<optimization>      - Performance improvements
style/<style-change>     - Code style changes
Docker Development
Build and Run Locally
Build the Docker image:

docker buildx bake --load
This creates an image tagged app:local using the configuration in docker-bake.hcl.

Run the container:

docker run -d -p 80:80 \
  -e BACKEND_URL=https://your-api.example.com \
  -e OAUTH_CLIENT_ID=your-id \
  -e OAUTH_CLIENT_SECRET=your-secret \
  --name my-react-app app:local

# View logs
docker logs -f my-react-app

# Stop the container
docker stop my-react-app && docker rm my-react-app
Visit http://localhost/ to see the app. API calls from the app will be proxied to BACKEND_URL with OAuth tokens.

Building with GitHub Token for Private Packages
export MY_GITHUB_TOKEN="ghp_xxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxx"
docker buildx bake --load --set *.args.MY_GITHUB_TOKEN=$MY_GITHUB_TOKEN
API Proxy and OAuth
How It Works
All API requests from the React app go to /_api/* on the same origin. The server (Express in production, Vite in dev) intercepts these requests and:

Strips the /_api prefix (e.g. /_api/items becomes /items)
If OAuth is configured, fetches a token using the client credentials grant and caches it until it expires
Adds Authorization: Bearer <token> to the request
Forwards the request to BACKEND_URL
This means the browser never sees the OAuth token -- it stays server-side.

Client-Side API Calls
The template includes an axios-based API client at app/src/api/client.ts:

import { getItems, createItem } from "@/api/client";

// GET request
const response = await getItems();
console.log(response.data);

// POST request
await createItem({ name: "My Item", description: "Optional description" });
All calls automatically go through the /_api proxy. Add your own API functions to client.ts.

Without OAuth
If OAUTH_CLIENT_ID or OAUTH_CLIENT_SECRET are not set, the proxy still works -- it just forwards requests without adding an Authorization header.

Environment Variables
Browser-Side (VITE_* prefix)
These are injected at container startup via env-config.js and available at window.__env__:

Variable	Default	Description
VITE_XYZ_MOCK_MODE	false	XYZ SDK mock mode
VITE_XYZ_WIDGET	false	XYZ SDK widget mode
VITE_XYZ_ENVIRONMENT_URL	http://localhost	XYZ environment URL
Server-Side (NOT exposed to browser)
Variable	Default	Description
BACKEND_URL	http://localhost:5000	Backend API URL to proxy to
OAUTH_TOKEN_URL	https://XYZ.[org].com/oauth2/token	OAuth2 token endpoint
OAUTH_CLIENT_ID	-	OAuth2 client ID
OAUTH_CLIENT_SECRET	-	OAuth2 client secret
OAUTH_AUDIENCE	default	OAuth2 audience
OAUTH_SCOPE	read write	OAuth2 scope
PORT	80 (prod) / 3001 (dev)	Server port
Build-Time
Variable	Description
MY_GITHUB_TOKEN	GitHub PAT for private packages

stla.gadp.docker.react.template/

Key Files
app/server.mjs: Production server -- Express serves static files and proxies /_api with OAuth
app/vite.config.ts: Dev server config -- same OAuth proxy behaviour via Vite plugin
app/src/api/client.ts: Axios instance -- all API calls go through /_api
app/src/App.tsx: Main component with routing
app/src/routes/ApiExample.tsx: Example page showing GET/POST API calls
docker/node20.11/Dockerfile: Multi-stage build -- Node.js replaces nginx in production
CI/CD Pipeline
How It Works
The CI/CD pipeline automatically:

Builds Docker images on every push
Tests the image can be built successfully
Publishes images to ghcr.io/[org]gadp/<repo-name> on merge to main
Tags with semantic versioning
Opens GitOps PRs to bump image tags in deployment/ values
Required Secrets for Private Packages
If you use private packages from GitHub Packages, configure this secret:

MY_GITHUB_TOKEN - GitHub PAT with read:packages scope
Add it at: Repository > Settings > Secrets and variables > Actions > New repository secret.

Versioning
Initial version: 0.0.1
Minor bump: Automatically on merge to main (e.g., 0.0.1 -> 0.0.2)
Major/Patch bump: Create a git tag
git tag v1.0.0
git push origin v1.0.0
Image Tags
ghcr.io/[org]gadp/<repo-name>:latest
ghcr.io/[org]gadp/<repo-name>:0.0.1
ghcr.io/[org]gadp/<repo-name>:main
Troubleshooting
OAuth / API Proxy Issues
Requests proxied without auth
If you see WARNING: OAUTH_CLIENT_ID or OAUTH_CLIENT_SECRET not set in logs, the proxy is forwarding requests without OAuth tokens. Set both OAUTH_CLIENT_ID and OAUTH_CLIENT_SECRET.

Token fetch fails
Check the logs for OAuth token request failed. Common causes:

Incorrect OAUTH_TOKEN_URL
Wrong OAUTH_CLIENT_ID / OAUTH_CLIENT_SECRET
Backend OAuth provider is unreachable from the container
502 Proxy Error
The Express proxy cannot reach BACKEND_URL. Verify:

BACKEND_URL is set correctly
The backend service is running and reachable from the container network
GitHub Packages Authentication Issues
404 Not Found or 401 Unauthorized
npm ERR! 404 Not Found - GET https://npm.pkg.github.com/@[org]gadp/package-name
Solutions:

Verify .npmrc has correct token (starts with ghp_)
Verify token has read:packages scope
Check token hasn't expired
Regenerate token if needed
npm install works locally but fails in Docker
export MY_GITHUB_TOKEN="ghp_xxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxx"
docker buildx bake --load --set *.args.MY_GITHUB_TOKEN=$MY_GITHUB_TOKEN
Node.js and npm Issues
Node version mismatch
node --version  # Should be 20.11+
nvm install 20 && nvm use 20
Port already in use
lsof -ti:3001 | xargs kill -9
# or use a different port:
npm run dev -- --port 3002
Dependencies installation fails
npm cache clean --force
rm -rf node_modules package-lock.json
npm install
Docker Build Issues
Build fails with "cannot find module"
Ensure all dependencies are in package.json
Check .dockerignore isn't excluding necessary files
Rebuild without cache: docker buildx bake --no-cache --load
Changes not reflecting in Docker
Rebuild and restart the container:

docker buildx bake --load
docker stop my-react-app && docker rm my-react-app
docker run -d -p 80:80 --name my-react-app app:local
Getting More Help
Check application logs:

# Docker
docker logs -f my-react-app
Verify versions:

node --version   # Should be 20.11+
npm --version    # Should be 10+
Contact your team for:

GitHub Packages access issues
Kubernetes deployment problems
CI/CD pipeline failures
Happy coding!

app/server.mjs:
import express from "express";
import { createProxyMiddleware } from "http-proxy-middleware";
import fs from "fs";
import path from "path";
import { fileURLToPath } from "url";

const __dirname = path.dirname(fileURLToPath(import.meta.url));
const DIST = path.join(__dirname, "dist");
/** Writable at runtime (K8s non-root); docker-entrypoint generates this. Falls back to dist/ for local images. */
const ENV_CONFIG_FILE =
  process.env.ENV_CONFIG_FILE || path.join("/tmp", "env-config.js");
const PORT = process.env.PORT || 80;

const BACKEND_URL = process.env.BACKEND_URL || "http://localhost:5000";
const TOKEN_URL =
  process.env.OAUTH_TOKEN_URL || "https://XYZ.Org.com/oauth2/token";
const CLIENT_ID = process.env.OAUTH_CLIENT_ID || "";
const CLIENT_SECRET = process.env.OAUTH_CLIENT_SECRET || "";
const AUDIENCE = process.env.OAUTH_AUDIENCE || "default";
const SCOPE = process.env.OAUTH_SCOPE || "read write";

const oauthEnabled = Boolean(CLIENT_ID && CLIENT_SECRET);

function log(tag, msg) {
  console.log(`[${new Date().toISOString()}] [${tag}] ${msg}`);
}

function logError(tag, msg, err) {
  console.error(`[${new Date().toISOString()}] [${tag}] ${msg}`, err ?? "");
}

let cached = null;
let pendingFetch = null;
let tokenFetchCount = 0;

async function fetchToken() {
  tokenFetchCount++;
  const attempt = tokenFetchCount;
  log("oauth", `Fetching new token (attempt #${attempt}) from ${TOKEN_URL}`);

  const basicAuth = Buffer.from(`${CLIENT_ID}:${CLIENT_SECRET}`).toString(
    "base64",
  );

  const res = await fetch(TOKEN_URL, {
    method: "POST",
    headers: {
      "Content-Type": "application/x-www-form-urlencoded",
      Authorization: `Basic ${basicAuth}`,
    },
    body: new URLSearchParams({
      grant_type: "client_credentials",
      scope: SCOPE,
      audience: AUDIENCE,
    }),
  });

  if (!res.ok) {
    const body = await res.text();
    throw new Error(
      `OAuth token request failed (attempt #${attempt}): ${res.status} – ${body}`,
    );
  }

  const data = await res.json();
  const expiresIn = data.expires_in;
  cached = {
    token: data.access_token,
    expiresAt: Date.now() + (expiresIn - 60) * 1000,
  };
  log(
    "oauth",
    `Token acquired (attempt #${attempt}), expires in ${expiresIn}s, ` +
      `cached until ${new Date(cached.expiresAt).toISOString()}`,
  );
  return cached.token;
}

async function getAccessToken() {
  if (cached && Date.now() < cached.expiresAt) return cached.token;
  log("oauth", "Token expired or missing, refreshing...");
  if (!pendingFetch) {
    pendingFetch = fetchToken().finally(() => {
      pendingFetch = null;
    });
  }
  return pendingFetch;
}

const app = express();

app.get("/env-config.js", (_req, res) => {
  const candidates = [ENV_CONFIG_FILE, path.join(DIST, "env-config.js")];
  for (const p of candidates) {
    if (fs.existsSync(p)) {
      res.type("application/javascript");
      res.setHeader("Cache-Control", "no-store");
      return res.sendFile(path.resolve(p));
    }
  }
  res.type("application/javascript");
  res.setHeader("Cache-Control", "no-store");
  return res.send("window.__env__ = {};\n");
});

app.use("/_api", async (req, _res, next) => {
  log("proxy", `${req.method} ${req.originalUrl} → ${BACKEND_URL}${req.url}`);
  if (oauthEnabled) {
    try {
      const token = await getAccessToken();
      req.headers["authorization"] = `Bearer ${token}`;
      log("proxy", "Authorization header injected");
    } catch (err) {
      logError("oauth", "Failed to obtain token:", err);
    }
  }
  next();
});

app.use(
  "/_api",
  createProxyMiddleware({
    target: BACKEND_URL,
    changeOrigin: true,
    pathRewrite: { "^/_api": "" },
    timeout: 10 * 60 * 1000,
    proxyTimeout: 10 * 60 * 1000,
    on: {
      proxyReq(proxyReq, req) {
        if (req.headers["content-length"]) {
          const sizeMB = (
            parseInt(req.headers["content-length"], 10) /
            1024 /
            1024
          ).toFixed(1);
          log("proxy", `Upload size: ${sizeMB} MB`);
        }
      },
      proxyRes(proxyRes, req) {
        log(
          "proxy",
          `← ${proxyRes.statusCode} ${req.method} ${req.originalUrl}`,
        );
      },
      error(err, req, res) {
        logError(
          "proxy",
          `Proxy error on ${req.method} ${req.originalUrl}:`,
          err.message,
        );
        if (!res.headersSent) {
          res.writeHead(502, { "Content-Type": "application/json" });
          res.end(
            JSON.stringify({ error: "Proxy error", message: err.message }),
          );
        }
      },
    },
  }),
);

app.use(express.static(DIST));

app.get("*", (_req, res) => {
  res.sendFile(path.join(DIST, "index.html"));
});

log("server", "=== React App Server ===");
log("server", `Node.js ${process.version}`);
log("server", `Static files: ${DIST}`);

if (!fs.existsSync(DIST)) {
  logError("server", `WARNING: dist directory does not exist at ${DIST}`);
} else {
  const fileCount = fs.readdirSync(DIST).length;
  log("server", `dist/ contains ${fileCount} top-level entries`);
}

log("server", `Backend URL: ${BACKEND_URL}`);
log("server", `OAuth enabled: ${oauthEnabled}`);
if (oauthEnabled) {
  log("oauth", `Token URL: ${TOKEN_URL}`);
  log("oauth", `Client ID: ${CLIENT_ID.slice(0, 8)}...`);
  log("oauth", `Audience: ${AUDIENCE}`);
  log("oauth", `Scope: ${SCOPE}`);
} else {
  log(
    "oauth",
    "WARNING: OAUTH_CLIENT_ID or OAUTH_CLIENT_SECRET not set – requests will be proxied without auth",
  );
}

const envConfigResolved =
  [ENV_CONFIG_FILE, path.join(DIST, "env-config.js")].find((p) =>
    fs.existsSync(p),
  );
if (envConfigResolved) {
  log(
    "server",
    `Runtime env-config.js: ${envConfigResolved} (${fs.statSync(envConfigResolved).size} bytes)`,
  );
} else {
  log(
    "server",
    "WARNING: env-config.js not found – VITE_ runtime vars will be empty",
  );
}

app.listen(PORT, () => {
  log("server", `Listening on port ${PORT}`);
  log("server", "Ready to accept connections");
});

app/vite.config.ts
import { defineConfig, loadEnv, type Plugin } from "vite";
import react from "@vitejs/plugin-react";
import path from "path";
import type { IncomingMessage, ServerResponse } from "http";

interface TokenCache {
  token: string;
  expiresAt: number;
}

function createOAuthProxyPlugin(env: Record<string, string>): Plugin {
  const tokenUrl =
    env.OAUTH_TOKEN_URL || "https://XYZ.Org.com/oauth2/token";
  const clientId = env.OAUTH_CLIENT_ID || "";
  const clientSecret = env.OAUTH_CLIENT_SECRET || "";
  const audience = env.OAUTH_AUDIENCE || "default";
  const scope = env.OAUTH_SCOPE || "read write";

  let cached: TokenCache | null = null;
  let pendingFetch: Promise<string> | null = null;

  const oauthEnabled = Boolean(clientId && clientSecret);

  async function fetchToken(): Promise<string> {
    const basicAuth = Buffer.from(`${clientId}:${clientSecret}`).toString(
      "base64",
    );

    const res = await fetch(tokenUrl, {
      method: "POST",
      headers: {
        "Content-Type": "application/x-www-form-urlencoded",
        Authorization: `Basic ${basicAuth}`,
      },
      body: new URLSearchParams({
        grant_type: "client_credentials",
        scope,
        audience,
      }),
    });

    if (!res.ok) {
      const body = await res.text();
      throw new Error(
        `OAuth token request failed: ${res.status} ${res.statusText} – ${body}`,
      );
    }

    const data = (await res.json()) as {
      access_token: string;
      expires_in: number;
    };

    cached = {
      token: data.access_token,
      expiresAt: Date.now() + (data.expires_in - 60) * 1000,
    };

    console.log(
      `[oauth-proxy] Token acquired, expires in ${data.expires_in}s`,
    );
    return cached.token;
  }

  async function getAccessToken(): Promise<string> {
    if (cached && Date.now() < cached.expiresAt) {
      return cached.token;
    }

    if (!pendingFetch) {
      pendingFetch = fetchToken().finally(() => {
        pendingFetch = null;
      });
    }
    return pendingFetch;
  }

  return {
    name: "oauth-proxy",
    configureServer(server) {
      if (!oauthEnabled) {
        console.log(
          "[oauth-proxy] OAUTH_CLIENT_ID / OAUTH_CLIENT_SECRET not set – proxy will forward requests without auth",
        );
        return;
      }

      console.log(`[oauth-proxy] Enabled – tokens from ${tokenUrl}`);

      server.middlewares.use(
        (
          req: IncomingMessage,
          _res: ServerResponse,
          next: (err?: unknown) => void,
        ) => {
          if (!req.url?.startsWith("/_api")) {
            next();
            return;
          }

          getAccessToken()
            .then((token) => {
              req.headers["authorization"] = `Bearer ${token}`;
              next();
            })
            .catch((err) => {
              console.error("[oauth-proxy] Failed to obtain token:", err);
              next();
            });
        },
      );
    },
  };
}

export default defineConfig(({ mode }) => {
  const env = loadEnv(mode, process.cwd(), "");

  const backendUrl = env.BACKEND_URL || "http://localhost:5000";

  return {
    base: "./",
    plugins: [react(), createOAuthProxyPlugin(env)],
    resolve: {
      alias: {
        "@": path.resolve(__dirname, "./src"),
      },
    },
    server: {
      port: 3001,
      proxy: {
        "/_api": {
          target: backendUrl,
          changeOrigin: true,
          rewrite: (p) => p.replace(/^\/_api/, ""),
        },
      },
    },
  };
});

docker/node20.11/Dockerfile
There's nothing called Dockerfile

docker-bake.hcl
variable "DEFAULT_TAG" {
  default = ["app:local"]
}

variable "MY_GITHUB_TOKEN" {
  default = ""
}

// Metadata-only target, not intended to build
target "docker-metadata-action" {
  tags = "${DEFAULT_TAG}"
}

// This target adds tags but does NOT define dockerfile/context
target "image" {
  inherits = ["docker-metadata-action"]
  context = "."
}

// Adds platforms but still not buildable alone
target "image-all" {
  inherits = ["image", "source-code", "node20-11"]
  platforms = [
    "linux/amd64",
    //"linux/arm64"
  ]
}

// Add additional contexts: --from=src
target "source-code" {
  contexts = {
    src = "cwd://app"
  }
}

// Concrete build targets with dockerfile & context
target "node20-11" {
  inherits = ["image-all"]
  dockerfile = "docker/node20.11/Dockerfile"
  args = {
    MY_GITHUB_TOKEN = "${MY_GITHUB_TOKEN}"
  }
}


// Combine multiple image targets into one group
target "image-local" {
  inherits = ["image", "source-code"]
  output = ["type=docker"]
  dockerfile = "docker/node20.11/Dockerfile"
  args = {
    MY_GITHUB_TOKEN = "${MY_GITHUB_TOKEN}"
  }
}

group "all-node" {
  targets = ["node20-11"]
}

// Default target to build
group "default" {
  targets = ["image-local"]
  args = {
    CI_BUILD_VERSION = "0.0.0+unknown"
  }
}

There's a readme in the docker/node20.11 folder:
# React Template Application

This repository is a Template for React applications that:

- Builds Docker images using Docker Buildx and `docker-bake.hcl`
- Publishes images to `ghcr.io/Orggadp/<name-of-repository>` via GitHub Actions
- Deploys to Kubernetes using Helm charts in `deployment/`
- Uses GitHub Personal Access Tokens (PAT) for private package access
- Built with React, TypeScript, and Vite for fast development
- Serves the production app with **Node.js / Express** (no nginx)
- Includes a built-in **API reverse proxy** (`/_api`) with **OAuth client credentials** token injection

---

## Table of Contents

- [Architecture](#architecture)
- [Quick Start](#quick-start)
- [Prerequisites](#prerequisites)
- [Local Development](#local-development)
- [Docker Development](#docker-development)
- [API Proxy and OAuth](#api-proxy-and-oauth)
- [Environment Variables](#environment-variables)
- [Project Structure](#project-structure)
- [CI/CD Pipeline](#cicd-pipeline)
- [Deployment](#deployment)
- [Troubleshooting](#troubleshooting)

---

## Architecture

The Docker container runs a **Node.js / Express** server (`server.mjs`) that:

1. **Serves static files** from the Vite `dist/` output
2. **Proxies API requests** from `/_api/*` to a configurable `BACKEND_URL` (stripping the `/_api` prefix)
3. **Injects an OAuth Bearer token** (client credentials flow) into proxied requests when `OAUTH_CLIENT_ID` and `OAUTH_CLIENT_SECRET` are set
4. **Falls back to `index.html`** for SPA routing

```
Browser
  ├── static assets ──→ Express serves dist/
  └── /_api/items   ──→ Express proxy ──(+Bearer token)──→ BACKEND_URL/items
```

In **development**, Vite provides the same behaviour: a custom plugin handles OAuth token injection and the built-in proxy forwards `/_api` requests to your backend.

---

## Prerequisites

### Required

- **Node.js 20.11+** (LTS recommended)
- **npm** (comes with Node.js) or **yarn**

### Optional (for Docker)

- **Docker Desktop** or Docker Engine (20.10+)
- **Docker Buildx** (included in Docker Desktop)

### Imporotant 

If you are planning to connect the frontend application with the backend and use it in XYZ, do not use a cookie-based approach.


### Private GitHub Packages

- **GitHub Personal Access Token (PAT)** with `read:packages` scope
- Required only if you need to install private packages from GitHub Packages

## Quick Start

### Using Private GitHub Packages (XYZ SDK)

> **Important for Deployment:** If you plan to deploy this application using CI/CD (not just run locally), you must also configure `MY_GITHUB_TOKEN` as a GitHub repository secret. See the [CI/CD section](#required-secrets-for-private-packages) below for instructions.

If your project requires private packages from GitHub Packages (like XYZ SDK):

**1. Create a GitHub Personal Access Token (PAT):**

- Navigate to GitHub > Settings > Developer settings > Personal access tokens > Tokens (classic)
- Generate a new token with **`read:packages`** scope
- Copy the token immediately (you won't see it again)

**2. Configure npm:**

```bash
cd app
cp .npmrc.example .npmrc
```

Edit `.npmrc`:

```ini
@Orggadp:registry=https://npm.pkg.github.com/
//npm.pkg.github.com/:_authToken=YOUR_GITHUB_TOKEN_HERE
```

**3. Get the app running:**

```bash
cd app
npm install
npm run dev
```

Visit `http://localhost:3001` to see your React app with hot reload!

## Local Development
### Use dev script (recommended)
```bash
./start_dev.sh              # macOS/Linux
./start_dev.ps1             # Windows
```
### Manual

```bash
cd app
npm install
npm run dev
```

The app starts on `http://localhost:3001` with instant hot module replacement (HMR).

To connect to a backend API during development, create an `.env` file in `app/`:

```bash
cp .env.example .env
```

Then set `BACKEND_URL` and optionally the `OAUTH_*` variables. The Vite dev server will proxy `/_api` requests to your backend with OAuth tokens injected automatically.

### Development Workflow

```bash
# 1. Create a new feature branch
git checkout -b feat/my-new-feature

# 2. Make changes to app/src files (Vite auto-reloads the browser)

# 3. Add new dependencies if needed
cd app
npm install new-package --save

# 4. Build for production (optional - to test build)
npm run build
npm run preview

# 5. Commit and push
git add .
git commit -m "feat: add new feature"
git push origin feat/my-new-feature
```

### Recommended Branch Naming

```text
feat/<feature-name>      - New features
fix/<bug-name>           - Bug fixes
refactor/<change-name>   - Code refactoring
docs/<doc-name>          - Documentation updates
test/<test-name>         - Adding tests
chore/<task-name>        - Maintenance tasks
perf/<optimization>      - Performance improvements
style/<style-change>     - Code style changes
```

## Docker Development

### Build and Run Locally

**Build the Docker image:**

```bash
docker buildx bake --load
```

This creates an image tagged `app:local` using the configuration in `docker-bake.hcl`.

**Run the container:**

```bash
docker run -d -p 80:80 \
  -e BACKEND_URL=https://your-api.example.com \
  -e OAUTH_CLIENT_ID=your-id \
  -e OAUTH_CLIENT_SECRET=your-secret \
  --name my-react-app app:local

# View logs
docker logs -f my-react-app

# Stop the container
docker stop my-react-app && docker rm my-react-app
```

Visit `http://localhost/` to see the app. API calls from the app will be proxied to `BACKEND_URL` with OAuth tokens.

### Building with GitHub Token for Private Packages

```bash
export MY_GITHUB_TOKEN="ghp_xxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxx"
docker buildx bake --load --set *.args.MY_GITHUB_TOKEN=$MY_GITHUB_TOKEN
```

---

## API Proxy and OAuth

### How It Works

All API requests from the React app go to `/_api/*` on the same origin. The server (Express in production, Vite in dev) intercepts these requests and:

1. Strips the `/_api` prefix (e.g. `/_api/items` becomes `/items`)
2. If OAuth is configured, fetches a token using the **client credentials** grant and caches it until it expires
3. Adds `Authorization: Bearer <token>` to the request
4. Forwards the request to `BACKEND_URL`

This means the **browser never sees the OAuth token** -- it stays server-side.

### Client-Side API Calls

The template includes an axios-based API client at `app/src/api/client.ts`:

```typescript
import { getItems, createItem } from "@/api/client";

// GET request
const response = await getItems();
console.log(response.data);

// POST request
await createItem({ name: "My Item", description: "Optional description" });
```

All calls automatically go through the `/_api` proxy. Add your own API functions to `client.ts`.

### Without OAuth

If `OAUTH_CLIENT_ID` or `OAUTH_CLIENT_SECRET` are not set, the proxy still works -- it just forwards requests without adding an Authorization header.

---

## Environment Variables

### Browser-Side (VITE_* prefix)

These are injected at container startup via `env-config.js` and available at `window.__env__`:

| Variable | Default | Description |
|----------|---------|-------------|
| `VITE_XYZ_MOCK_MODE` | `false` | XYZ SDK mock mode |
| `VITE_XYZ_WIDGET` | `false` | XYZ SDK widget mode |
| `VITE_XYZ_ENVIRONMENT_URL` | `http://localhost` | XYZ environment URL |

### Server-Side (NOT exposed to browser)

| Variable | Default | Description |
|----------|---------|-------------|
| `BACKEND_URL` | `http://localhost:5000` | Backend API URL to proxy to |
| `OAUTH_TOKEN_URL` | `https://XYZ.Org.com/oauth2/token` | OAuth2 token endpoint |
| `OAUTH_CLIENT_ID` | - | OAuth2 client ID |
| `OAUTH_CLIENT_SECRET` | - | OAuth2 client secret |
| `OAUTH_AUDIENCE` | `default` | OAuth2 audience |
| `OAUTH_SCOPE` | `read write` | OAuth2 scope |
| `PORT` | `80` (prod) / `3001` (dev) | Server port |

### Build-Time

| Variable | Description |
|----------|-------------|
| `MY_GITHUB_TOKEN` | GitHub PAT for private packages |

```
│   │   ├── App.tsx              # Main App component with routing

│   │   │   └── client.ts       # Axios API client (calls /_api proxy)

│   │   └── vite-env.d.ts       # TypeScript types for window.__env__
│   │
│   ├── server.mjs               # Production Express server (proxy + OAuth)
│   ├── public/                   # Static assets
│   ├── index.html                # HTML template (loads env-config.js)
│   ├── package.json              # Node.js dependencies
│   ├── vite.config.ts            # Vite config (OAuth dev proxy plugin)
│   ├── tsconfig.json             # TypeScript configuration
│   ├── tsconfig.app.json         # App TypeScript config (with @ path alias)
│   ├── eslint.config.js          # ESLint configuration
│   ├── .env.example              # Example environment variables
│   └── .npmrc.example            # Example npm configuration
│
├── deployment/                   # Kubernetes deployment configs
│   └── ee/
│       └── dev/
│           └── values.yaml      # Helm values for dev environment
│
├── docker/
│   └── node20.11/
│       ├── Dockerfile           # Multi-stage build (Node.js, not nginx)
│       └── docker-entrypoint.sh # Generates env-config.js, starts Node
│
├── .github/
│   └── workflows/               # CI/CD workflows
│       ├── docker-bake-ci.yaml
│       └── re-seal-sealed-secrets.yaml
│
├── config/
│   └── ci-config.yaml           # CI tooling versions / config
│
├── docker-bake.hcl              # Docker Buildx bake configuration
├── .dockerignore                # Files to exclude from Docker builds
├── .gitignore                   # Files to exclude from Git
└── README.md                    # This file
```

### Key Files

- **`app/server.mjs`**: Production server -- Express serves static files and proxies `/_api` with OAuth
- **`app/vite.config.ts`**: Dev server config -- same OAuth proxy behaviour via Vite plugin
- **`app/src/api/client.ts`**: Axios instance -- all API calls go through `/_api`
- **`app/src/App.tsx`**: Main component with routing
- **`app/src/routes/ApiExample.tsx`**: Example page showing GET/POST API calls
- **`docker/node20.11/Dockerfile`**: Multi-stage build -- Node.js replaces nginx in production

---

## CI/CD Pipeline

### How It Works

The CI/CD pipeline automatically:

1. **Builds** Docker images on every push
2. **Tests** the image can be built successfully
3. **Publishes** images to `ghcr.io/Orggadp/<repo-name>` on merge to `main`
4. **Tags** with semantic versioning
5. **Opens GitOps PRs** to bump image tags in `deployment/` values

### Required Secrets for Private Packages

If you use private packages from GitHub Packages, configure this secret:

- `MY_GITHUB_TOKEN` - GitHub PAT with `read:packages` scope

Add it at: Repository > Settings > Secrets and variables > Actions > New repository secret.

### Versioning

- **Initial version**: `0.0.1`
- **Minor bump**: Automatically on merge to `main` (e.g., `0.0.1` -> `0.0.2`)
- **Major/Patch bump**: Create a git tag

```bash
git tag v1.0.0
git push origin v1.0.0
```

### Image Tags

```
ghcr.io/Orggadp/<repo-name>:latest
ghcr.io/Orggadp/<repo-name>:0.0.1
ghcr.io/Orggadp/<repo-name>:main
```

---

## Troubleshooting

### OAuth / API Proxy Issues

#### Requests proxied without auth

If you see `WARNING: OAUTH_CLIENT_ID or OAUTH_CLIENT_SECRET not set` in logs, the proxy is forwarding requests without OAuth tokens. Set both `OAUTH_CLIENT_ID` and `OAUTH_CLIENT_SECRET`.

#### Token fetch fails

Check the logs for `OAuth token request failed`. Common causes:
- Incorrect `OAUTH_TOKEN_URL`
- Wrong `OAUTH_CLIENT_ID` / `OAUTH_CLIENT_SECRET`
- Backend OAuth provider is unreachable from the container

#### 502 Proxy Error

The Express proxy cannot reach `BACKEND_URL`. Verify:
- `BACKEND_URL` is set correctly
- The backend service is running and reachable from the container network

### GitHub Packages Authentication Issues

#### 404 Not Found or 401 Unauthorized

```
npm ERR! 404 Not Found - GET https://npm.pkg.github.com/@Orggadp/package-name
```

Solutions:

1. Verify `.npmrc` has correct token (starts with `ghp_`)
2. Verify token has `read:packages` scope
3. Check token hasn't expired
4. Regenerate token if needed

#### npm install works locally but fails in Docker

```bash
export MY_GITHUB_TOKEN="ghp_xxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxx"
docker buildx bake --load --set *.args.MY_GITHUB_TOKEN=$MY_GITHUB_TOKEN
```

### Node.js and npm Issues

#### Node version mismatch

```bash
node --version  # Should be 20.11+
nvm install 20 && nvm use 20
```

#### Port already in use

```bash
lsof -ti:3001 | xargs kill -9
# or use a different port:
npm run dev -- --port 3002
```

#### Dependencies installation fails

```bash
npm cache clean --force
rm -rf node_modules package-lock.json
npm install
```

### Docker Build Issues

#### Build fails with "cannot find module"

1. Ensure all dependencies are in `package.json`
2. Check `.dockerignore` isn't excluding necessary files
3. Rebuild without cache: `docker buildx bake --no-cache --load`

#### Changes not reflecting in Docker

Rebuild and restart the container:

```bash
docker buildx bake --load
docker stop my-react-app && docker rm my-react-app
docker run -d -p 80:80 --name my-react-app app:local
```

### Getting More Help

1. **Check application logs:**
   ```bash
   # Docker
   docker logs -f my-react-app
   ```

2. **Verify versions:**
   ```bash
   node --version   # Should be 20.11+
   npm --version    # Should be 10+
   ```

3. **Contact your team for:**
   - GitHub Packages access issues
   - Kubernetes deployment problems
   - CI/CD pipeline failures

That could be even cleaner operationally, but requires more integration work.

20. So the plan I'd use
I would not recreate the XYZ app.

I'd proceed in this order:

Phase 1 — establish SpecPilot truth
Use Copilot to answer:

how it starts
how it builds
how the API works
how frontend ↔ backend connects
what Docker actually does
what environment variables exist
whether anything external is required
Phase 2 — establish XYZ truth
From the actual XYZ-generated repository, determine:

exact CI workflow
exact Docker build command
Docker build context
generated Dockerfile
what PORT must do
whether XYZ expects one process or can route to a second process
whether app/ is genuinely required or merely generated convention
what XYZ regenerates
what files we must not modify
Phase 3 — create a mapping
Something like:

SpecPilot	XYZ target	Treatment
web/src	app/src	Move/copy
server/src	app/server/src	Move/copy
shared/src	app/shared/src or root shared	Decide from build
tests	tests	Preserve
root package.json	app/package.json or root	Decide from build
vite.config.ts	app/vite.config.ts	Adapt paths
tsconfig.json	XYZ equivalent	Adapt paths
vitest.config.ts	XYZ/root	Adapt paths
Dockerfile	XYZ Docker strategy	Adapt/rewrite
deploy/smoke.sh	deployment validation	Preserve/adapt
docs/*	documentation	Preserve
core/*	application logic	Do not rewrite










Phase 4 — migrate mechanically
Only after the mapping is proven.

Phase 5 — verify equivalence
Run:

existing tests
        ↓
frontend build
        ↓
backend build
        ↓
Docker build
        ↓
container startup
        ↓
health/API smoke tests
        ↓
XYZ deployment
        ↓
XYZ smoke test










That gives us a controlled migration instead of letting XYZ "interpret" SpecPilot.

Bottom line
No, I would not delete the XYZ app and start again. The Node/Vite choice is appropriate given the actual SpecPilot repository.

And I would not select a database, LLM, or Flask backend unless the source-code audit proves SpecPilot needs one.

More importantly, I think we should stop thinking of this as "moving a Vite app into XYZ." Your actual system is:

React/Vite frontend + Node/Express API + deterministic generation core + shared contracts + comprehensive tests, wrapped in a Docker/Kubernetes deployment environment.

The XYZ-generated app/server.mjs is mostly deployment/runtime plumbing; it is not your SpecPilot backend.

The next step I'd take is the Copilot audit above, particularly the Docker audit and the root package.json/tsconfig/vitest/Dockerfile inspection. Once you paste those results here, we can produce a file-by-file migration map and decide whether web/, server/, and shared/ should actually be moved under app/ or whether we can leave them at root and simply adapt the XYZ build. That is much safer than making the structural change now.

Also, the fact that ChatGPT can reason over connected GitHub repository contents is useful here: GitHub-connected repositories can be read as live repository data rather than relying solely on pasted snippets.

Yes — this is exactly a planning/analysis task, not a builder task.

And now that you have found the Dockerfile, we have a useful concrete clue: the current project is definitely a Node.js application at the container level, using Node 20, with a build producing dist, and a small Node/Express production server (server.mjs) that also uses http-proxy-middleware. I would not tell Copilot that the frontend framework is definitely Vite/React/etc. unless it discovers that from package.json and the source.

Which agent?
Use Planner, not Auditor.

Planner: determine whether this can be migrated to XYZ, what has to change, what XYZ expects, and what the viable migration architectures are.
Auditor: useful afterward to independently challenge the plan and identify assumptions/gaps.
Builder: absolutely not needed yet. Don't let it start rewriting code before you know whether the target architecture is even compatible.
In fact, I'd have Planner produce MigrationAnalysis0.md, then give that analysis to your Auditor agent for a hostile/independent review.

Is Claude Sonnet 5 enough?
Yes. Sonnet 5 is more than sufficient for this first-pass investigation. Anthropic describes it as substantially improved over Sonnet 4.6 in reasoning, tool use, coding, and agentic work, and specifically positions it for complex multi-step software-engineering tasks.

Anthropic
+1

You don't need to throw Opus/Fable at this unless the Planner gets stuck on some genuinely complicated XYZ/platform architecture question. The important thing here is giving the agent the repository, the XYZ findings, the Dockerfile, and permission to investigate rather than prematurely prescribing a solution.

And yes: be explicitly open to recreating the application from scratch. That's important. Otherwise the agent may contort the existing architecture just to preserve it.

Here is the prompt I'd give your Planner. I have deliberately written it so it tells the agent what we actually know, while forcing it to distinguish facts from assumptions.

XYZ Migration Architecture Investigation
You are the Planner agent for a migration feasibility and architecture investigation.

Your job at this stage is NOT to write or modify application code. Your job is to thoroughly inspect the existing repository, understand the current application and its deployment assumptions, investigate the target XYZ deployment model using the available repository documentation, and produce a technically grounded migration analysis and plan.

Your final output must be:

MigrationAnalysis0.md

Do not make implementation changes to the application as part of this task.

1. Overall objective
XYZ is the company's internal application/platform in which I will ultimately deploy the current locally developed project after refactoring and improving it.

The code currently lives in this repository and was developed locally.

The platform team has created a separate repository intended for deployment through XYZ. We need to determine how the current application can be moved/adapted into that deployment repository and successfully deployed as a XYZ application.

I need your analysis and plan for making this work.

I am completely open to:

Migrating the existing application with minimal changes.
Refactoring/restructuring the existing application substantially.
Adapting the application to a XYZ-specific application model.
Recreating the application from scratch in the XYZ repository if that is ultimately simpler, safer, or more maintainable.
Do not assume that preserving the existing implementation is inherently preferable.

The goal is a successful, maintainable XYZ deployment — not preservation of the existing architecture for its own sake.

2. Important: inspect the repository before forming conclusions
First inspect the repository thoroughly.

At minimum, investigate:

package.json
lock files
complete source structure
build configuration
application entry points
frontend/backend structure
environment/configuration handling
API/proxy behavior
static assets
scripts
tests
documentation
existing deployment/configuration files
Docker-related files
CI/CD configuration
any references to GitHub Packages
any references to external/internal registries
any assumptions about ports
any assumptions about filesystem paths
any runtime environment assumptions
Also inspect:

docs/WhatIFoundAboutXYZ.md

This document contains information I have already gathered about the XYZ environment, including the target repository/platform structure and deployment model.

Treat that document as investigation evidence, not necessarily as absolute truth.

If anything in it conflicts with what you discover elsewhere in the repository or in the target deployment repository, explicitly identify the conflict.

3. Current Dockerfile evidence
I have found the following Dockerfile in the current project:

# Build Stage
FROM node:20.11-alpine AS build

WORKDIR /app

ARG MY_GITHUB_TOKEN

This gives us some concrete evidence about the current application, but do not infer more than the evidence supports.

What we can currently say with reasonable confidence is:

The application uses Node.js.
The build stage uses Node 20.11-alpine.
The production stage uses Node 20 Alpine.
npm install is used.
The application has an npm run build step.
The build output is expected at dist/.
server.mjs is used at runtime.
The production image installs Express 4 and http-proxy-middleware 3.
The container exposes port 80.
There is a custom docker-entrypoint.sh.
There is currently some support for installing packages from GitHub Packages using MY_GITHUB_TOKEN.
There is a CI_BUILD_VERSION build/runtime variable.
The current deployment approach therefore involves custom Docker/container behavior.
However, do not assume that all of this must remain true under XYZ.

4. Critical XYZ assumption
One of the key things we need to establish is:

XYZ itself will handle the Docker/container baking and containerisation/deployment mechanics.

Therefore, the current Dockerfile should NOT automatically be treated as something that must be copied into the XYZ deployment repository.

Determine exactly which responsibilities currently handled by:

the Dockerfile
Docker build stages
docker-entrypoint.sh
npm installation
environment variables
exposed ports
runtime server configuration
will instead be handled by XYZ.

Identify which responsibilities remain application responsibilities.

a Node application with a particular project structure;
